1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design-8.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=P3i6Rzvl 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/Untitled%20design-8.jpg.webp?h=6a4a5c49\u0026itok=utTKQv6n"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe deepfake story you think you know is incomplete.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen most people hear “deepfake,” they picture a fake face.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne important nuance: \u003cstrong\u003ea deepfake face does not automatically mean a scam\u003c/strong\u003e, and a scam does not even require a deepfake face at all. Visuals can be a useful clue, but \u003cstrong\u003eidentifying synthetic media is getting more complex\u003c/strong\u003e, and simplistic “looks fake” judgments are increasingly unreliable. That is why we lean on \u003cstrong\u003emultiple signals\u003c/strong\u003e, including audio analysis and transcript, to reason about intent and behavior.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eConnecting the term deepfake with deceit makes sense, it is the most visual part of the problem, and it has become a cultural shorthand for deception. But in real-world scam campaigns, the face is often just packaging. The engine is usually the message, including the script, the call to action, the pressure, and the promise.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat's why our detection starts where the persuasion lives: the content, especially the audio. Scam videos often reuse the same pitches, again and again, such as “guaranteed returns,” “limited-time access,” “you’ve been selected,” “act before it’s too late.” Even when the visuals look polished, even when the footage looks familiar, the audio can give away the intent.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs we continued our research on what makes a deepfake a scam, we started asking another question that changed what we looked for:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOnce a video is already labeled as scam content by our audio and content analysis, which faces keep showing up across those scam videos?\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe answer was not only “famous people.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt was actually a set of VIPs that do not exist.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy we looked for “VIPs” in scam video\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn scam ecosystems, repetition is rarely an accident. Criminals optimize what works, then scale it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo instead of asking “who is this person?” we asked “how often does this face appear across scam-labeled videos, and where does it show up?” If the same presenter is repeatedly used as a delivery vehicle for scam scripts, that recurrence becomes a signal of reuse, coordination, and industrialization.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo do that, our proof of concept takes scam-labeled videos, extracts faces, generates compact visual descriptors, then groups them by similarity. The most prevalent identities in that scam-labeled set become “VIPs,” meaning faces that appear unusually often across scam content. We use faces here as an additional \u003cstrong\u003esignal \u003c/strong\u003efor grouping and speed, to connect related scam videos and spot scale. It complements audio and transcript analysis, it is not the primary factor deciding whether a video is a scam.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe expected the usual outcome: a list dominated by high-profile public figures that scammers frequently impersonate.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd yes, those faces are there. Public figures like Elon Musk have become recurring bait in investment-themed scams, giveaways, and “exclusive opportunity” narratives.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBut the most important finding was what appeared alongside the celebrity targets.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eVIPs that don’t exist\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSome of the most prevalent “presenters” in scam video are not real people.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThey look like ordinary talking-head hosts: friendly expression, neutral styling, camera-ready delivery. They speak clearly, they project confidence, and they often appear in exactly the kind of format people have learned to trust: a calm explainer, a “news-style” segment, a professional endorsement, a casual recommendation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBut they are AI avatars, synthetic presenters generated to be reused. To be clear, an AI avatar is not automatically a scam; what makes these specific presenters relevant is that we found them recurring inside videos already labeled as scams based on the message and behavior.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn general, proving that a person on screen is synthetic can require detailed and expensive analysis, things like lip-sync consistency, motion artifacts, and other forensic techniques. Our approach is different: we identify and aggregate recurring avatar identities from scam-labeled videos using lightweight visual descriptors and similarity grouping. That gives us a faster, cheaper way to recognize AI avatar presenters that are not yet “known,” but are already showing up frequently in the wild.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe are confident about this classification. These are not ambiguous “maybe enhanced” clips. They are AI avatar outputs, and we have also seen the supply chain behind them, including avatar creation services marketed openly on freelance marketplaces like Fiverr.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis matters because it changes the threat model.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you only think about deepfakes as “someone impersonating a celebrity,” you miss the bigger shift: scammers are increasingly manufacturing credibility without impersonating anyone at all.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat might sound like a minor distinction, but it changes the incentives.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e908326041bf0d633ca65ffb02170a711\"\u003eA celebrity deepfake creates obvious controversy and is easier for platforms and victims to challenge.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3d8efe40c9161bf5c06bb597444c608c\"\u003eA synthetic presenter has no “real person” attached, no victim to complain, and no public figure to debunk the content.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efe3688ea8aebb1aa6c655308f6223fda\"\u003eThe same synthetic presenter can be reused endlessly, across topics and languages, with minimal cost.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn short, these “VIPs” are not famous because they are recognized, they are VIPs because they are operational assets in scam production.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFrom celebrity deepfakes to avatar farm\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCelebrity impersonation is still here, and it will remain attractive. Reputation is a powerful shortcut to trust.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBut impersonation has trade-offs. It is high-profile, easier to report, and riskier for scammers. It can also be brittle, a single debunk can collapse a campaign.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSynthetic presenters reduce that risk. You do not have to mimic a specific person, you just have to look credible enough to keep someone listening for 20 seconds.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is why the “avatar farms” mental model fits.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eInstead of crafting a one-off forgery, scammers can run a production line:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ebb598c8a5f36ed5bf22c58a25cf5137d\"\u003eCreate or acquire a synthetic presenter that looks broadly trustworthy\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e037f34075f6fe1c6b325444d26aec9ad\"\u003eGenerate scripts at volume, tailored to whatever is trending\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9830311305c72262b00d5e0ed4706f87\"\u003eSwap voiceovers, captions, and visual overlays\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1829309715a175d81fbb484eaf94b1e0\"\u003ePublish, measure engagement, then publish again with minor variations\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat production line scales. It is designed for experimentation, iteration, and reach.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd it works because most scams do not require cinematic realism. They require momentum.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eSynthetic trust, the new social engineering\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHumans are wired to treat faces and voices as social proof. A confident presenter can make a claim feel less risky, even when the claim is outrageous. A calm delivery can make urgency feel “reasonable.” A familiar format can make a stranger’s instructions feel like a normal next step.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the core idea behind synthetic trust.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI avatars are not trying to be memorable. They are trying to be acceptable. They are engineered to pass a quick gut-check, “this seems legitimate,” long enough to drive a click, a download, a payment, or a message.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor scammers, that is an upgrade. A synthetic presenter does not get tired. It does not demand a fee. It does not have a moral objection. It can be generated in many languages, in endless variations, and deployed in parallel across platforms.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou may still see celebrity bait at the top of the funnel. But underneath it, a lot of scam content is shifting toward manufactured presenters that are easier to scale.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe twist: sometimes the video is real and only the audio is weaponized\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is another reason audio-first detection matters: not every scam video relies on a fake face.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSometimes criminals take legitimate footage, a real interview, a real tutorial, a real product explainer, and overlay a scam pitch on top. The visuals can be authentic. The deception sits in the narration, the instructions, and the call to action.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis technique does two things for scammers:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee6d78ff507a6dd63751daeb5224bcc60\"\u003eIt reduces production cost, because they can recycle existing footage\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eae4e6dba3c1d54936aa4100bfd420af0\"\u003eIt reduces suspicion, because the visuals look “normal” at a glance\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf defenses focus only on “does the face look fake?”, this kind of repurposed content can slip through. That is why we treat the message as primary. Then, once content is labeled as scam, we look for the repeated visual infrastructure that carries those messages, including recurring AI avatar presenters.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA note on hired actors\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eNot every recurring presenter in scam content is synthetic. We have also seen scams that use hired actors or commissioned videos. Sometimes it is a human reading a script, sometimes it is a human clip later repurposed with different narration or overlays.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn other words: the “avatar farm” trend does not replace all old techniques. It sits alongside them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is also why we avoid simplistic rules like “this face is fake therefore it is a scam.” What matters is intent and behavior. Faces help us connect the dots faster as a signal, they are not the verdict.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003ePractical tips: how to protect yourself from scam videos\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf a video is pushing you toward money, logins, downloads, or urgent verification, treat the presenter as part of the packaging, not proof.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003col type=\"1\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"eb375588bdd69b963a569da0423a5860d\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFollow the action, not the face.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eIf the video is urging urgency, secrecy, or immediate payment, pause. That pressure is often the scam.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecf36de53c2869ca44dd7de68b8dd6a10\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eVerify outside the platform.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eSearch for the claim from independent sources, not links in the description or comments.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea8e5ddb75021374558a5553272959836\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBe suspicious of “too smooth” certainty.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eScam scripts often sound confident, generic, and strangely frictionless, as if risk does not exist.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee5e84990c119d747dd4cb8566a61b415\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eWatch for mismatch.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eIf the visuals feel unrelated to the pitch, or the audio feels pasted-on, treat it as a warning sign.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ecd31fbfea4a6ecebbceff95dfbcc3458\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAvoid installs prompted by videos.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e“Install this to verify,” “download this viewer,” “run this command,” these are common routes into malware and credential theft.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e190568cd7906f183381254eeea433387\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUse protection tools.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cbr\u003eTools like Norton’s AI-powered Scam Protection and Avast Deepfake Guard can help spot what’s easy to miss, including deepfake audio in videos and scam signals across web, SMS and more.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe bottom line\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp lang=\"EN-US\"\u003eDeepfakes may get the headlines, but they are only part of the story. From impersonating real people to manufacturing trust at scale, faces, voices, scripts, formats, all of it can be generated, reused and optimized.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp lang=\"EN-US\"\u003eWhich means the real skill is not spotting what looks fake; it is recognizing how scams behave.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp lang=\"EN-US\"\u003eFollow the pressure. Question the promise. Verify outside the starts with a message that does not feel wrong.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou have a family trip coming up. You made the booking days or weeks ago, saved the confirmation and moved on with your life. Then WhatsApp pings.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe message says it is from the hotel’s Guest Relations team. It references the same property you booked. It mentions details that match your stay. It sounds like the kind of practical note you might expect before a trip, a small inconsistency, a quick verification, a routine check before arrival.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd that is exactly why it works.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a scam contains real details, it stops feeling like spam and starts feeling like customer service. The attacker does not need perfect writing. They do not need malware. They do not even need a particularly clever pretext. They just need enough truth to make the lie feel safe.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the pattern at the heart of what we are calling the \u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/blog/online-scams/reservation-hijacking-scam\" target=\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eReservation Hijack Scam\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe call it a \u003cstrong\u003eReservation Hijack Scam\u003c/strong\u003e because the attacker is not just spoofing a hotel or sending a generic phishing lure. They are hijacking the context around a real reservation and using it to make fraud feel like routine customer service.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the stronger versions of the attack, that goes further. Once hotel staff credentials are phished, the attacker may gain access to real hotel or partner accounts and contact guests from within legitimate workflows, for example through a booking platform account or hotel messaging environment. At that point, the scam is no longer only borrowing the hotel’s identity, it is actively impersonating the hotel through systems the guest already trusts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the shift that matters most. This is not just a travel-themed phishing lure dressed up with better wording. In the strongest cases, criminals can move from impersonating the hotel to operating through compromised hotel-side or partner workflows, contacting real travelers in the context of real reservations.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA scam category built on stolen and relayed trust\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost phishing is generic. It arrives with no context, so your brain treats it with suspicion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReservation Hijack scams work differently. They reflect real context back at the victim, the hotel name, the destination, the fact that there is a genuine booking, sometimes the stay window, sometimes even the exact amount due. That reflected truth becomes the bypass.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAdd a short fuse, usually 24 or 48 hours, and the attacker gets what they want most, fast compliance before the victim pauses, verifies or calls the property.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is not just a nicer phishing template. That is what makes the scam feel different from ordinary phishing. The victim is not chosen at random. They are contacted in the context of a real trip, with details that match an actual booking.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe level of personalization depends on how much information the attacker has. When they only have limited data, such as an email address or phone number, the scam may look more generic, but it can still exploit hotel branding, urgency and the expectation of pre-trip communication.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat difference is visible in the lures themselves. In high-context variants, the message may name the guest, reference a specific stay date and amount, or lead to a payment page already populated with reservation details. In the stronger version, the attacker may message the guest from a real hotel or booking-related account they have taken over. The difference is important. The victim may not just see a plausible hotel message, they may see what looks like a legitimate continuation of their existing booking conversation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn one case we reviewed, the phishing message was delivered through a legitimate Booking.com partner communication thread after the accommodation partner account had been compromised. The malicious request was injected into an otherwise normal conversation, making the attack especially difficult to detect because it arrived from a trusted source rather than a spoofed sender. The follow-up warning from the hotel highlights the confusion and damage that can occur once a real partner account is abused:\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=V8RBsqNr 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=LcTJw8nF 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":843,"height":2069},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=Cv2U4dxO 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=0N5UdXba 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":843,"height":2069},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=K26Q5Psq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=yzKFVDXm 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":1693}],"caption":"Phishing injected into a legitimate Booking.com message thread","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/email-01.jpg.webp?itok=K26Q5Psq","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThen comes the message. It usually arrives over a fast, informal channel such as WhatsApp or SMS, and it is written to feel administrative rather than alarming. The goal is not to scare the victim, but to make the request feel routine, a payment issue, a booking confirmation, a verification step or an urgent action before arrival.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the examples we reviewed, the exact wording and format varied by platform, but the underlying pattern stayed consistent: the message borrowed hotel context, introduced a practical problem, and pushed the guest toward a verification or payment step that benefited the attacker.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom there, the victim is pushed into a controlled flow. That may be a fake guest portal, a spoofed payment page, or even a branded PDF that adds one more layer of credibility before the victim reaches the real theft stage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe theft happens when the traveler enters payment details, approves a transfer or otherwise complies with the fake verification process. At that point, the attacker has turned stolen trust into financial fraud.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd the damage may not stop there. Payment details can be reused, tested with small charges, sold or leveraged in follow-on scams. What begins as a believable hotel message can end in broader financial compromise.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is what makes this scam different. It is not just phishing with a travel theme. It is a workflow attack built on stolen context and relayed trust.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Cloudbeds path, step by step\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCloudbeds has publicly warned of an industry-wide phishing campaign targeting hospitality providers and described credential-phishing attacks impersonating Cloudbeds login pages as the root cause rather than a Cloudbeds platform breach.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat point matters especially for small and medium-sized hospitality businesses. Large travel brands usually have bigger security teams, more formalized controls and more mature fraud processes. Smaller properties often rely on lean teams, fast guest communication and a handful of operational platforms to keep everything moving. That makes them particularly vulnerable when attackers target employees directly with credential phishing.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attack chain we mapped is brutally efficient.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFirst, hotel staff are lured to fake login pages and their credentials are stolen. Then the attacker logs into the real management environment. From there, they can see future reservations, guest names, contact details, stay information and sometimes payment context. In some scenarios, that access may also extend beyond visibility. If the compromised hotel workflow is connected to guest-facing messaging on booking or hospitality platforms, the attacker may be able to contact customers from the property’s legitimate account presence. That makes the fraud especially dangerous, because the message may arrive inside a channel the traveler already associates with their real reservation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the cases documented in our working material, some victims received SMS messages containing detailed reservation information and links that led to professionally styled PDFs impersonating hotel groups. Those PDFs pushed “payment verification” within 48 hours, using generic branding, urgency, and a prominent call to action to move the victim toward the real theft stage. The PDF step appears to function as a buffer, one more layer of legitimacy for the victim, and one more layer of indirection for scanners and filters to deal with. In some cases, the PDF itself was hosted on legitimate partner storage that had been hijacked, giving the lure another borrowed layer of trust before redirecting the victim onward.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=FsgHRmxW 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=wIKnhQ7r 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1012,"height":1094},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=v_a6UT7P 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=dlnsQLcq 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":962},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=nSE_MyF6 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=ykCCn1dc 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":746}],"caption":"Victim is redirected to typo-squatted domains designed to harvest card details, bank transfers or other payment information","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-03/pdf-01.jpg.webp?itok=nSE_MyF6","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eFrom there, the victim is redirected again, typically to typo-squatted domains designed to harvest card details, bank transfers or other payment information. Example domains documented in the shared material include frontdesk-reservation[.]com, frontdesk-online[.]biz, and hotel.form842987[.]digital.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOne scam, multiple surfaces\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the part many people miss.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe WhatsApp message, the SMS, the fake PDF, the lookalike payment page, the stolen card, these are not separate incidents. They are one pipeline. And that pipeline can include direct impersonation of the hotel itself. Sometimes the attacker stands outside the workflow and imitates the property. Sometimes the attacker steps inside a compromised hotel or booking-related account and speaks from a position the guest already trusts. Different surface, same scam family.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFirst, the attacker gets context. Next, they use that context to deliver a believable message through a channel people act on quickly. Then they move the victim into a verification flow that feels administrative, not dangerous. If payment details are entered, the harm does not stop at one fraudulent charge. Cards can be tested with small transactions, then used for larger purchases, then resold or retried later.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is what makes the scam so dangerous. The same fraud logic can be adapted to different platforms, different levels of stolen context, and different points in the guest journey.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy this matters now\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eHospitality has become an unusually attractive environment for this kind of fraud.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHotels run on urgency, constant guest communication and high-trust operational workflows. Travelers expect messages about check-in, balances, room preferences, arrival timing, and identity verification. That makes it easier for attackers to hide malicious intent inside language that would look normal in almost any other context.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd when the attacker is working with real reservation data, or worse, using compromised hotel or booking-linked accounts to contact guests directly, the old consumer advice starts to bend. Checking the link still matters, but it is no longer enough on its own. The victim is not just being tricked by branding. They are being tricked by context.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat travelers should do\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf a hotel contacts you via Booking.com messaging, Airbnb, WhatsApp, SMS or email and asks you to verify payment details, do not tap the link. The same caution applies if the message appears to come from an existing reservation thread or from what looks like the hotel’s real account on a booking platform. Trust the booking, not the message.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGo to the booking site, hotel site, or official app yourself. If you need to call the property, use a trusted contact route from the original booking or the verified website, not the number or link inside the message.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you already entered payment details, assume compromise. Contact your bank, cancel the card, enable transaction alerts and watch for follow-on attempts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat hotels should learn from this\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe lesson for hospitality is not just “train staff better,” although that matters. It is that hotel software, messaging workflows, and guest communication tools now sit directly on the fraud path.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe hotel is no longer only a victim. In the eyes of the guest, it can become the face of the scam. In the worst cases, it can also become the voice of the scam, when attackers use compromised hotel or partner accounts to message guests as if they were legitimate staff.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat means account protection, phishing-resistant authentication, tighter controls around exports and guest messaging, anomaly detection and faster incident response are no longer nice-to-haves. They are part of brand protection.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBecause once attackers learn how to turn operational systems into trust amplifiers, they do not need to break in loudly. They can simply log in, relay stolen trust to the guest, and let the workflow do the rest.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eOne final thought\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor years, the best advice on travel scams was simple: watch for bad grammar, generic messages, and suspicious links.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat advice still matters. But it belongs to an earlier phase of the problem.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe next phase looks like this: the message knows your booking, sounds plausible, and may arrive through a channel or account the traveler already trusts. That is the point where customer service and fraud begin to overlap.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the Reservation Hijack Scam.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the next part of this series, we will look more closely at the infrastructure, indicators and investigative signals behind it.\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"15","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-03/hero.jpg?h=dab30fcb\u0026itok=6dgmVnYL","language":"en","heroImage":{"default":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_hero_extra_wide/public/2026-03/hero.jpg.webp?h=dab30fcb\u0026itok=57IRtf3Q
